## Page 189


E.1. Universes sharing a common algorithmic structure
Recall that in the proof of Theorem E.22, in order to show that there are (fully-supported) policies
and environments sharing arbitrary large degrees of structural similarity according to the Solomonoff
prior, we constructed a family M𝜏
sim of (fully-supported) universes in which policies and environments
share a common structure, and then we showed that inside M𝜏
sim we can always find 𝜆= 𝜈𝜋for which
the degree of structural similarity between 𝜈and 𝜋according to the Solomonoff prior is arbitrarily
large.
This fact does not depend on the particular form of the structure which is shared among all universes
in M𝜏
sim. In this section, we formalize a general notion of "algorithmic structure" and then show that
as long as this structure satisfies a few conditions, then we can always find policies and environments
respecting this structure and which share arbitrarily large degrees of structural similarity with respect
to the Solomonoff prior.
Intuitively, an algorithmic structure can be understood as a "boilerplate template" structure that can
be respected by multiple computer programs. Let us consider the following two programs
Algorithm 11: Pseudocode using the subroutine function 𝑓(𝑥) = 𝑥+ 1
Function f(𝑥):
return 𝑥+ 1;
Function main_func(𝑥):
𝑦←0;
for 𝑖←1 to 𝑥do
𝑦←f(𝑦+ f(𝑥));
return 𝑦;
Algorithm 12: Pseudocode using the subroutine function 𝑓(𝑥) = 𝑥2
Function f(𝑥):
return 𝑥2;
Function main_func(𝑥):
𝑦←0;
for 𝑖←1 to 𝑥do
𝑦←f(𝑦+ f(𝑥));
return 𝑦;
As one can see, the main function main_func has a similar algorithmic structure in both programs,
as it uses the subroutine function f in the same way, and they differ only by the specification of 𝑓.
We will use this idea to define a notion of an algorithmic structure that can be shared by a family
of universes. We will basically define a fixed "boilerplate template" structure which refers to some
subroutine that is not specified, and we will define the family of all universes that can be implemented
by an algorithm that respects the "boilerplate template" structure. Different specifications of the
subroutine yield different universes in the family.
The reader might have noticed that the notion of subroutine is very similar to the notion of an oracle
that can be called by an oracle machine, and this is indeed how we will formalize this notion.
For this, we need to introduce machines that can use more than one oracle.
189


## Page 190


Definition E.24 (Multi-Oracle Machines). An 𝑛-abstract probabilistic oracle machine (𝑛-APOM)
is a monotone Turing machine equipped with 𝑛distinct oracle tapes, denoted 𝑇1, . . . , 𝑇𝑛, and a
corresponding set of 𝑛distinct query instructions, 𝑄1, . . . , 𝑄𝑛. When instruction 𝑄𝑖is executed, the
machine interacts with oracle tape 𝑇𝑖.
An 𝑛-probabilistic oracle machine (𝑛-POM) is a pair (𝑀, (𝜏1, . . . , 𝜏𝑛)), where 𝑀is an 𝑛-APOM and
each 𝜏𝑖: {0, 1}∗→[0, 1] is a probabilistic oracle123 . When instruction 𝑄𝑖is called, the content of
the tape 𝑇𝑖is interpreted as a query which is answered by oracle 𝜏𝑖and the content of the tape 𝑇𝑖is
replaced with the answer of the oracle. The standard APOM and POM are equivalent to a 1-APOM
and 1-POM, respectively.
Each of the 𝑛probabilistic oracles can be understood as a distinct, black-box subroutine that the
main program (the 𝑛-APOM) can call. The 𝑛-APOM defines the high-level logic or "template", while
the specific oracles provide the implementation details for its sub-components.
Remark E.25. While the above definition only uses probabilistic oracles that return single random
bits, the definition is general enough to model probabilistic oracles that can return random bit strings.
Basically, if 𝑂: {0, 1}∗→Δ{0, 1}∗is a probabilistic oracle that returns random bit strings, we can
effectively represent it using two probabilistic oracles, 𝜏𝑒: {0, 1}∗→[0, 1] and 𝜏𝑏: {0, 1}∗→[0, 1],
where:
• 𝜏𝑒(⟨𝑥, 𝑦⟩) represents the probability that 𝑂(𝑥) = 𝑦conditioned on 𝑦being a prefix of 𝑂(𝑥).
• 𝜏𝑏(⟨𝑥, 𝑦⟩) represents the probability that 𝑂(𝑥) = 𝑦1 conditioned on 𝑦being a strict prefix of
𝑂(𝑥).
The following pseudocode illustrates how we can use 𝜏𝑒and 𝜏𝑏to simulate sampling from 𝑂:
Algorithm 13: Sampling from 𝑂using 𝜏𝑒and 𝜏𝑏
Input: A bit string 𝑥∈{0, 1}∗.
Output: A random sample from 𝑂(𝑥)
𝑦←𝜀
while True do
if 𝑂𝜏𝑒(⟨𝑥, 𝑦⟩) = 1 then
return 𝑦;
else if 𝑂𝜏𝑏(⟨𝑥, 𝑦⟩) = 1 then
𝑦←𝑦1;
else
𝑦←𝑦0;
The framework with multiple oracles allows us to compose machines. An 𝑛-APOM can be reduced to
a machine with fewer free subroutines by specifying what some of its oracles are. For instance, if we
fix a base probabilistic oracle 𝜏, we can construct a complex 1-POM by using an 𝑛-APOM where the
first oracle remains 𝜏, but the other 𝑛−1 oracles are themselves implemented by other 1-POMs that
also rely on 𝜏. The resulting composite machine is a single, more complex 1-POM that depends only
on the base oracle 𝜏.
123Recall that if 𝑥is the query to the oracle 𝜏𝑖, the oracle returns 1 with probability 𝜏𝑖(𝑥), and returns 0 with probabilty
1 −𝜏𝑖(𝑥).
190


## Page 191


More formally, we can think of this composition syntactically. Given an 𝑛-APOM, 𝑀, and 𝑛−1 standard
1-APOMs, 𝑀2, . . . , 𝑀𝑛, we can construct a new 1-APOM, denoted 𝑀[𝑀2, . . . , 𝑀𝑛]. This new machine
simulates 𝑀as follows:
• When 𝑀calls its first oracle, 𝑄1, the new machine passes this query to its own single oracle.
• When 𝑀calls any other oracle 𝑄𝑖(for 𝑖∈{2, . . . , 𝑛}), it instead simulates the execution of the
corresponding machine 𝑀𝑖, feeding the query string as input to 𝑀𝑖and using the output of 𝑀𝑖
as the oracle’s answer. While executing the machine 𝑀𝑖, any call to the (single) oracle of 𝑀𝑖is
directed to the single oracle of the new machine.
This allows us to formally define the notion of a shared algorithmic structure.
Definition E.26 (Algorithmic Structure). An 𝑛-APOM, 𝑀, defines an algorithmic structure. A
1-APOM, 𝑀′, is said to possess the structure defined by 𝑀if there exist 𝑛−1 other 1-APOMs,
𝑀2, . . . , 𝑀𝑛, such that 𝑀′ is computationally equivalent to the composite machine 𝑀[𝑀2, . . . , 𝑀𝑛].
Now we can define specific types of algorithmic structures for policies, environments and universes.
Definition E.27 (Algorithmic Structure for policies). An algorithmic structure for policies is an
𝑛-APOM, 𝑀, that is syntactically constrained to take as inputs valid encodings ⟨æ∗⟩from AE∗and
produce outputs ⟨𝑎⟩with 𝑎∈A. We say that a policy 𝜋: AE∗→Δ′A possesses the structure 𝑀with
respect to a probabilistic oracle 𝜏: {0, 1}∗→[0, 1] if there exists a 1-APOM 𝑀𝜋such that
• 𝜋(𝑎| æ∗) = ℙ[𝑀𝜏
𝜋(⟨æ∗⟩) = ⟨𝑎⟩], with ℙ[𝑀𝜏
𝜋(⟨æ∗⟩) = ⟨𝑎⟩] denoting the probability that 𝑀𝜏
𝜋
outputs ⟨𝑎⟩upon input ⟨æ∗⟩, and
• 𝑀𝜋possesses the structure defined by 𝑀.
We denote the set of policies that possess the structure 𝑀with respect to 𝜏as M𝜏
pol(𝑀).
Definition E.28 (Algorithmic Structure for environments). An algorithmic structure for environ-
ments is an 𝑛-APOM, 𝑀, that is syntactically constrained to take as inputs ⟨æ∗, 𝑎⟩with (æ∗, 𝑎) ∈
AE∗× A and produce outputs ⟨𝑒⟩with 𝑒∈E. We say that an environment 𝜈: AE∗× A →Δ′E

1-APOM 𝑀𝜈such that
• 𝜈(𝑒| æ∗𝑎) = ℙ[𝑀𝜏

𝜈(⟨æ∗, 𝑎⟩) = ⟨𝑒⟩] denoting the probability that 𝑀𝜏
𝜈
outputs ⟨𝑒⟩upon input ⟨æ∗, 𝑎⟩, and
• 𝑀𝜈possesses the structure defined by 𝑀.
We denote the set of environments that possess the structure 𝑀with respect to 𝜏as M𝜏
env(𝑀).
Definition E.29 (Algorithmic Structure for universes). An algorithmic structure for universes is an
𝑛-APOM, 𝑀, that does not have any inputs, and writes sequences from B# on its output tape. We say
that a semimeasure 𝜎: B∗→[0, 1] possesses the structure 𝑀with respect to a probabilistic oracle 𝜏
if there exists a 1-APOM 𝑀𝜎such that

𝑀𝜎, and
• 𝑀𝜎possesses the structure defined by 𝑀.

Definition E.31 (Proper subroutines). A POM 𝑀𝜏is said to be a proper subroutine if for every input
𝑥∈B∗, the POM 𝑀𝜏almost surely halts with some 𝑦∈B written on the output tape. In other words,
𝑀𝜏induces a mapping 𝑀𝜏: B∗→ΔB.
Definition E.32 (Distributional equivalence of POMs). Let 𝑀1 and 𝑀2 be two 1-APOMs. We say that

We write 𝑀𝜏
1 ≡𝑀𝜏
2 to denote the distributional equivalence of 𝑀𝜏
1 and 𝑀𝜏
2.
Definition E.33 (Identifiable subroutines). Let 𝑀be an 𝑛-APOM. We say that the algorithmic structure
𝑀has identifiable subroutines with respect to the probabilistic oracle 𝜏if there are 𝑛−1 2-APOMs
𝐼2, . . . , 𝐼𝑛such that for every 𝑛−1 1-APOMs 𝑀2, . . . , 𝑀𝑛for which 𝑀𝜏

and every 2 ≤𝑖≤𝑛, the POMs 𝐼𝑖[𝑀[𝑀2, . . . , 𝑀𝑛]]𝜏≡𝑀𝜏

we have
ℙ[𝐼𝑖[𝑀[𝑀2, . . . , 𝑀𝑛]]𝜏(𝑥) = 𝑦] = ℙ[𝑀𝜏
𝑖(𝑥) = 𝑦] .
In other words, if we had access to an oracle that on any query 𝑥returns samples from 𝑀[𝑀2, . . . , 𝑀𝑛]𝜏(𝑥),

Let us construct a simple example to illustrate the concept of subroutine identifiability:
Example E.34 (A simple example of an Algorithmic Structure with Identifiable Subroutines). Consider
the algorithmic structure defined by the following 2-APOM 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡:

Algorithm 14: Algorithmic Structure 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡
Input: An input string 𝑥∈{0, 1}∗.
Output: A canonical encoding of a pair of strings ⟨𝑦, 𝑧⟩.
𝑦←𝑄2(𝑥);
𝑧←𝑄1(𝑦);
return ⟨𝑦, 𝑧⟩;
This structure has "identifiable subroutines" because we can construct an "identifier" machine, 𝐼2, that
can recover the behavior of any proper subroutine 𝑀2 that may be plugged into the second oracle slot
of 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡.
Algorithm 15: Identifier machine 𝐼2
Input: An input string 𝑥∈{0, 1}∗.
Output: A string 𝑦.
𝑠←𝑄2(𝑥);
Decode 𝑠into a pair ⟨𝑦, 𝑧⟩;
return 𝑦;
Now, let us verify that 𝐼2 can indeed identify any subroutine specification for the second oracle
𝑄2 of 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡. Let 𝑀2 be an arbitrary 1-APOM. We need to show that the 1-POM 𝐼2[𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡[𝑀2]]𝜏is
distributionally equivalent to 𝑀𝜏
2. Let’s trace the execution of 𝐼2[𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡[𝑀2]]𝜏on an input 𝑥:
1. The machine begins by simulating 𝐼2. The first step of 𝐼2 is to call its second oracle, 𝑄2, with
input 𝑥.
2. According to the composition rule, this call to 𝐼2’s second oracle is replaced by a simulation of
the machine plugged into that slot, which is 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡[𝑀2]. This simulation is run with the base
oracle 𝜏.
3. So, we execute 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡[𝑀2]𝜏(𝑥). This machine, in turn, first simulates 𝑀2 with the oracle 𝜏. Let
the probabilistic output of 𝑀𝜏
2(𝑥) be the string 𝑦𝑜𝑢𝑡.
4. Next, 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡[𝑀2]𝜏calls its first oracle (which is passed through to the base oracle 𝜏) with 𝑦𝑜𝑢𝑡.
Let the result be 𝑧𝑜𝑢𝑡. It then outputs the pair ⟨𝑦𝑜𝑢𝑡, 𝑧𝑜𝑢𝑡⟩.
5. This pair is the result returned to 𝐼2 from its oracle call in step 1.
6. 𝐼2 then proceeds to its second step: It decodes the pair ⟨𝑦𝑜𝑢𝑡, 𝑧𝑜𝑢𝑡⟩and outputs the first element,
which is 𝑦𝑜𝑢𝑡.
The final output is 𝑦𝑜𝑢𝑡, which is precisely the probabilistic output of 𝑀𝜏
2(𝑥). Therefore, the machines
are distributionally equivalent, and we have successfully "identified" or extracted the behavior of the
subroutine 𝑀2.
The structure 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡in the above example has identifiable subroutines because it explicitly includes
the output of its subroutines in its own final output. This "pass-through" design is a straightforward
way to ensure that the behavior of sub-components is not obscured by subsequent computations.
Nevertheless, there are more complex algorithmic structures that can still have identifiable subroutines,
as illustrated in the following example:

Example E.35 (A Slightly More Complex Example of an Algorithmic Structure with Identifiable
Subroutines). Consider the algorithmic structure defined by the following 3-APOM 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡:
Algorithm 16: A Slightly More Complex Algorithmic Structure 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡
Input: An input string 𝑥∈{0, 1}∗.
Output: A canonical encoding of a pair ⟨𝑟, 𝑦⟩or ⟨𝑥′, 𝑦⟩.
if 𝑥= 𝜀then
Flip a random coin 𝑟∈{0, 1};
if 𝑟= 0 then
𝑦←𝑄2(𝜀);
else
𝑦←𝑄3(𝜀);
return ⟨𝑟, 𝑦⟩;
else
Draw a random string 𝑥′ ∈{0, 1}𝑙(𝑥) ;
𝑥′′ ←𝑥⊕𝑥′ ; // We compute the bit-wise XOR between 𝑥and 𝑥′.
if the number of ones in 𝑥′ is even then
𝑦←𝑄2(𝑥′′);
else
𝑦←𝑄3(𝑥′′);
return ⟨𝑥′, 𝑦⟩;
The structure 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡also has identifiable subroutines, although extracting the subroutines requires a
more sophisticated process than simply decoding the output. To identify 𝑀𝜏
2, we must repeatedly
sample from the composite machine 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡[𝑀2, 𝑀3]𝜏and filter for the cases where the random string
𝑥′ has an even number of ones.
The following pseudocode illustrates the algorithm of 𝐼2.
Algorithm 17: Identifier machine 𝐼2
Input: An input string 𝑥𝑖𝑛∈{0, 1}∗.
Output: A random sample from 𝑀𝜏
2(𝑥𝑖𝑛)
if 𝑥𝑖𝑛= 𝜀then
while True do
Call the second oracle 𝑄2 with input 𝑥𝑖𝑛= 𝜀. Let the result be 𝑠;
Decode 𝑠as a pair ⟨𝑟, 𝑦⟩;
if 𝑟= 0 then
return 𝑦;
else
while True do
Draw a random string 𝑥∈{0, 1}𝑙(𝑥𝑖𝑛) using the internal randomness tape;
Call the second oracle 𝑄2 with input 𝑥. Let the result be 𝑠;
Decode 𝑠as a pair ⟨𝑥′, 𝑦⟩;
if 𝑥⊕𝑥′ = 𝑥𝑖𝑛and the number of ones in 𝑥′ is even then
return 𝑦;
An analogous machine 𝐼3 can be designed by checking for 𝑟= 1 when 𝑥𝑖𝑛= 𝜀, and checking for an
194


## Page 195


odd number of ones in 𝑥′ in case 𝑥𝑖𝑛≠𝜀.
To prove that the structure is identifiable, we must show that 𝐼2[𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡[𝑀2, 𝑀3]]𝜏is distributionally
equivalent to 𝑀𝜏
2. We analyze the two cases based on the input 𝑥𝑖𝑛to 𝐼2.
• Case 1: 𝑥𝑖𝑛≠𝜀. The machine 𝐼2 enters a loop where it draws a random string 𝑥of the same
length as 𝑥𝑖𝑛and queries its second oracle (the composite machine 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡[𝑀2, 𝑀3]𝜏) with this 𝑥.
The composite machine, in turn, draws another random mask 𝑥′ and calls either 𝑀2 or 𝑀3 with
the input 𝑥′′ = 𝑥⊕𝑥′. The identifier 𝐼2 accepts the result 𝑦only if the number of ones in 𝑥′ is
even (indicating 𝑀2 was called) and if 𝑥⊕𝑥′ = 𝑥𝑖𝑛. Since both 𝑥and 𝑥′ are drawn uniformly at
random, the resulting input 𝑥′′ to the subroutine is also uniformly random over strings of that
length. Therefore, for any target 𝑥𝑖𝑛, the condition 𝑥′′ = 𝑥𝑖𝑛will eventually be met. When it is,
the returned 𝑦is a correct sample from 𝑀𝜏
2(𝑥𝑖𝑛).
• Case 2: 𝑥𝑖𝑛= 𝜀. The machine 𝐼2 enters a simpler loop. It repeatedly queries its second oracle
with the empty string 𝜀. In response, the composite machine 𝑀𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑢𝑐𝑡[𝑀2, 𝑀3]𝜏flips a coin 𝑟. If
𝑟= 0, it calls 𝑀2(𝜀); otherwise, it calls 𝑀3(𝜀). It returns the pair ⟨𝑟, 𝑦⟩. The identifier 𝐼2 waits
until it receives a pair where 𝑟= 0. Since the coin flip is fair, this is guaranteed to happen
eventually. When it does, the corresponding 𝑦is a correct sample from 𝑀𝜏
2(𝜀).
In both cases, the identifier machine 𝐼2 successfully simulates a sample from 𝑀𝜏
2(𝑥𝑖𝑛) using its oracle
access to the composite machine. This process of filtering executions is a form of rejection sampling.
Thus, the structure has identifiable subroutines.
With this general framework for algorithmic structures, we can now state the main result of this
section.
Theorem E.36. For every fixed choice of:
• a canonical encoding ⟨𝑀⟩of APOMs as binary strings,
• a universal monotone Turing machine 𝑈,
• a probabilistic oracle 𝜏(which may or may not be a RUI-oracle),

F. Multi-agent environments and their relation to universes
Here, we provide some additional details on multi-agent environments and how to ‘embed’ agent
policies by interfacing them with this multi-agent environment.

G. A few desiderata for a Bayesian theory of embedded universal intelligence
This section formulates a set of desiderata which, we believe, a good Bayesian theory of embedded
universal intelligence should satisfy. The desiderata that we specify here capture the issues of AIXI
199


## Page 200


and JAIXI that we informally described in Appendix B.8, and which made them fail in embedded
settings.
It is worth noting that the desiderata that we specify in this section are not meant to capture every
aspect of embedded agency. There are several aspects of embedded agency which were mentioned in
Demski and Garrabrant (2019) and which we do not capture in the desiderata that we specify here
(e.g., we do not consider here the possibility that the policy of an agent may be self-modified by the
agent, or modified by the environment). Our aim here is mainly to mathematically and rigorously
formalize a set of goals which capture some of the aspects described in Demski and Garrabrant (2019)
and for which AIXI and JAIXI fail. This helps motivate our theory of Embedded Universal Predictive
Intelligence (MUPI), as well as provide intuition and clarify the motivation behind the mathematical
concepts in our MUPI theory.
We start by formulating one set of desiderata that captures a few embeddedness aspects from a purely
single-agent perspective:125
Problem G.1. (Informal) We aim to find three classes Mpol, Menv, Muni and a universal prior 𝑤∈
Δ′Muni such that the following desiderata are satisfied:
1. Generality of the considered policies: Mpol is a countable class of policies (i.e., policy-like
chronological conditional semimeasures) which contains all computable policies.
2. Generality of the considered environments: Menv is a countable class of environments
(i.e., environment-like chronological conditional semimeasures) which contains all computable
environments.
3. Generality of the considered universes: Muni is a countable class of semimeasures on AE∗
which contains all computable semimeasures on AE∗. The class Muni can be thought of as a
class of (distributions of histories of) interactions between an agent and an environment. We
will use the term "universe" to refer to a semimeasure on AE∗which can be seen as jointly
describing an agent and an environment.
4. The interaction between a considered policy and a considered environment yields a
universe that is considered in Muni: For every 𝜋∈Mpol and every 𝜈∈Menv, we have
𝜈𝜋∈Muni.
5. Embedded Bayesian agents with respect to the Muni hypothesis class are part of the
considered policies: There exists a policy 𝜋∗∈Mpol which performs "a form of universal
embedded Bayesian optimization" as detailed in Sections 3.3, 3.4 and 4.4 with respect to the
mixture universe
𝜌:=
∑︁
𝜆∈Muni
𝑤(𝜆)𝜆.
Notice how the desiderata in the above problem captures the issue that we mentioned about JAIXI in
B.8: JAIXI is not lower semicomputable, and hence if we let it interact with a computable environment,
we may get a universe which is not lower semicomputable, i.e., we get something which is outside
the hypothesis class of JAIXI. Putting this in the language of the above desiderata: If Mpol contains
JAIXI and Menv contains all lower semicomputable semimeasures, then Muni must contain universes
125We later formulate a more general set of desiderata which capture some embeddedness aspects from a multi-agent
perspective. The more general set of desiderata will be able to capture the notion that a single-agent environment, as seen
from the perspective of one agent, may contain/embed other agents which may be implementing similar policies to the
policy of the considered agent.
200


## Page 201


which are not lower semicomputable, and hence the hypothesis class of JAIXI would not cover all the
universes in Muni, i.e., JAIXI would not be a valid solution of the last point of Problem G.1.
Now given a solution to Problem G.1, if we make the policy 𝜋∗interact with an environment in Menv,
then the fourth point implies that we get a universe which is in the hypothesis class Muni of the
"embedded Bayesian" policy 𝜋∗. This is in contrast with JAIXI which does not necessarily give rise to
a lower semicomputable interaction if we make it interact with a computable environment.
Now we are ready to state our desiderata for a good theory of embedded universal intelligence, from
a multi-agent perspective:

G.1. The MUPI theory satisfies our desiderata
By inspecting Problems G.1 and G.2, we can see that we essentially have all the ingredients:
• We can take Muni to be the class of all universes which are equipped with a 𝑤-RUI-oracle 𝜏,
where 𝑤∈Δ′MrAPOM is some lower semicomputable prior on the class of restricted abstract
probabilistic oracle machines MrAPOM. Note that Muni is countable and contains all lower
semicomputable semimeasures on B∗.
• For finite sets A, O and R with prefix-free encodings thereof, we can take the class of policies
M A,O,R

encoding ⟨æ1:𝑡⟩of a history æ1:𝑡∈AE∗and produces the prefix-free encoding ⟨𝑎⟩of an action
𝑎∈A.

• As shown by Theorem 5.31, the set of considered policies contains approximate embedded
Bayesian agents: All (𝑘𝑡, 𝜖𝑡)𝑡-E-AIXIRUI agents are members of the policy classes defined above.
Furthermore, the approximations of these agents can be made arbitrarily good.
• The interaction between a considered policy and a considered single agent environment (fourth
point in Problem G.2) does indeed yield a universe in Muni: One just needs to take the machines
𝑃and 𝐸and build a machine 𝑀which makes 𝑃and 𝐸interact by alternately exchanging inputs
and outputs appropriately. We omit the technical details here.
– The fifth point of Problem G.2 regarding the reduction of a 𝑘-multi-agent environment into
a (𝑘−ℓ)-multi-agent environment by fixing ℓpolicies for ℓagents, can be shown similarly.
We can also solve G.1 and G.2 using the reflective oracle framework by considering the classes of all
policies (resp., environment, 𝑘-multi-agent environments, universes) which are implementable using
POMs with access to a reflective oracle 𝜏. Theorem 5.29 shows that the universal embedded Bayesian
agents E-AIXIRO and 𝑘-E-AIXIRO for 𝑘> 0 are all implementable as 𝜏-POMs.
